120	
121	\response{We have added a footnote to Table~1 explaining the discrepancy: ``Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.'' The value 0.100 is the exact result; 0.102 is a rounding artifact. We now use 0.100 consistently throughout.}
122	
123	\location{Table~1 footnote.}
124	
125	\subsection*{Q5: Single checkpoint per configuration}
126	
127	\reviewer{Each model zoo configuration is a single training run. Can you disentangle architecture from seed?}
128	
129	\response{We cannot, and we now state this explicitly in the Limitations section: ``Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.'' This is a limitation of the zoo design. Disentangling the two would require multiple seeds per architecture-attention configuration, which is beyond the scope of the available zoo. We note, however, that the zoo's controlled design (shared data, hyperparameters) reduces other confounds, making architecture the most salient axis of variation.}
130	
131	\location{Limitations (``Architecture coverage'' paragraph).}
132	
133	\subsection*{W-REFS (R2-W2): Additional related work citations}
134	
135	\reviewer{Several relevant references are missing from the related work discussion.}
136	
137	\response{We have added Sellam et al.\ (ICLR 2022; MultiBERTs) and Fehlauer et al.\ (EMNLP 2025) to the Related Work section (\S2). Additional references are under verification and will be incorporated if confirmed before the camera-ready deadline.}
138	
139	\location{\S2 (``Underspecification and Model Agreement'' paragraph), \texttt{references.bib}.}

    "snippet": "1\t\\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n2\t\n3\t\\subsection*{A. For every submission:}\n4\t\n5\t\\begin{itemize}\n6\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{A1.} Did you describe the limitations of your work?\n7\t\n8\t\\textit{Yes. The Limitations section (after \\S5) discusses statistical power constraints ($n = 5$ attention ratios), per-example scope (two benchmarks), scale coverage (two scale points), architecture coverage (three backbones from one model zoo, single training run per configuration), observational design (descriptive, not causal), and pre-training-only evaluation.}\n9\t\n10\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{A2.} Did you discuss any potential risks of your work?\n11\t\n12\t\\textit{Yes. The Ethics Statement notes that this work analyzes existing public models and introduces no new risks. Per-example behavioral differences may inform practitioners about limitations of aggregate benchmarks.}\n13\t\n14\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{A3.} Do all claims in the paper match the paper's contributions and scope?\n15\t\n16\t\\textit{Yes. Claims are scoped to three architectures and two benchmarks. We explicitly note that Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not reach conventional significance, that the scale comparison is a preliminary observation from two data points, and that per-example multiplicity is ``observed across retrieval and reasoning tasks'' rather than claimed as universal.}\n17\t\n18\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{A4.} Have you used AI writing assistants when working on this paper?\n19\t\n20\t\\textit{Yes. AI writing assistants were used for drafting and editing. All scientific content, claims, and analyses were verified by the authors.}\n21\t\\end{itemize}\n22\t\n23\t\\subsection*{B. Did you use or create scientific artifacts?}\n24\t\n25\t\\textit{Yes. We use existing artifacts (pretrained models, evaluation framework).}\n26\t\n27\t\\begin{itemize}\n28\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{B1.} Did you cite the creators of artifacts you used?\n29\t\n30\t\\textit{Yes. We cite the model zoo \\citep{wang2025hybrid}, the evaluation framework \\citep{gao2024lmeval}, and all individual architecture papers (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2).}\n31\t\n32\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{B2.} Did you discuss the license or terms for use and/or distribution of any artifacts?\n33\t\n34\t\\textit{The model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} is publicly released. The lm-evaluation-harness \\citep{gao2024lmeval} is open-source (MIT license). We do not release new artifacts.}\n35\t\n36\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{B3.} Did you discuss if your use of existing artifact(s) was consistent with their intended use?\n37\t\n38\t\\textit{Yes. The model zoo was designed for systematic comparison of hybrid architectures, which is exactly our use case. The evaluation harness was used with default prompts for standard benchmarks.}\n39\t\n40\t\\item[N/A] \\textbf{B4.} Did you discuss the steps taken to check whether the data contains information that names or uniquely identifies individual people or offensive content?\n41\t\n42\t\\textit{Not applicable. We use established NLP benchmarks (LAMBADA, ARC-Easy, etc.) that have been widely used in prior work. No new data was collected.}\n43\t\n44\t\\item[N/A] \\textbf{B5.} Did you provide documentation of the artifacts?\n45\t\n46\t\\textit{Not applicable. We do not release new artifacts.}\n47\t\n48\t\\item[N/A] \\textbf{B6.} Did you discuss whether and how consent was obtained from people whose existing data you're using/curating?\n49\t\n50\t\\textit{Not applicable. All benchmarks used are established public datasets.}\n51\t\\end{itemize}\n52\t\n53\t\\subsection*{C. Did you run computational experiments?}\n54\t\n55\t\\textit{Yes. We ran inference-only evaluation (no training).}\n56\t\n57\t\\begin{itemize}\n58\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{C1.} Did you report the number of parameters in the models used, the total computational budget, and computing infrastructure?\n59\t\n60\t\\textit{Yes. We report model sizes (340M and 1.3B parameters) and the number of configurations evaluated (30 models $\\times$ 11 benchmarks). All evaluation was inference-only using the pretrained checkpoints of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}; no model training was performed. Inference was conducted on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU with a total computational budget of approximately 5 GPU-hours.}\n61\t\n62\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{C2.} Did you discuss the experimental setup, including hyperparameter search and best model selection?\n63\t\n64\t\\textit{Yes. \\S3 describes the model zoo, task taxonomy, and all metrics. All models use default lm-evaluation-harness settings with no hyperparameter tuning or model selection.}\n65\t\n66\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{C3.} Did you report descriptive statistics about your results?\n67\t\n68\t\\textit{Yes. We report exact permutation tests, BCa bootstrap confidence intervals, Bonferroni and Benjamini--Hochberg corrections, effect sizes (Cohen's $d$, Jaccard similarity, normalized ES), and the ambiguity metric $\\alpha$ of \\citet{marx2020predictive}.}\n69\t\\end{itemize}\n70\t\n71\t\\subsection*{D. Did you use human annotators?}\n72\t\n73\t\\textit{No.}\n74\t\n75\t\\begin{itemize}\n76\t\\item[N/A] \\textbf{D1--D5.} \\textit{Not applicable. No human annotation was involved in this study.}\n77\t\\end{itemize}\n78\t\n79\t\\subsection*{E. Did you use AI assistants?}\n80\t\n81\t\\begin{itemize}\n82\t\\item[\\checkmark] \\textbf{E1.} Did you report the use of AI assistants?\n83\t\n84\t\\textit{Yes; see A4 above.}\n85\t\\end{itemize}"

[Director 指令] [Patch — MAJOR: 4-Benchmark Upgrade]

BoolQ + HellaSwag per-example 分析完成。论文从 2→4 benchmarks 升级，覆盖全部 4 个 task category。这是提交前最后一次重大内容变更。

### 新数据

**4 Benchmark 汇总**（替换原 Table 2）：

| Benchmark | Category | N | α | J̄_h | J̄_u | ρ_σ |
|-----------|----------|-------|------|------|------|------|
| LAMBADA | Retrieval | 5153 | .30 | .086 | .091 | -1.0* |
| BoolQ | Comprehension | 3270 | .49 | .103 | .098 | +0.1 |
| HellaSwag | Reasoning | 10042 | .10 | .091 | .080 | -0.8 |
| ARC-Easy | Mixed | 2376 | .21 | .092 | .096 | -0.2 |

*p=.017

**核心 narrative 升级**：Multiplicity 不是 "存在/不存在" 的 binary 发现，而是 task-dependent spectrum（10%→49%）。HellaSwag 作为 "零效应参照" 排除 noise 解释。

### 需要修改的位置

**Title**: 保持 "Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures"（无需 qualifier，4 benchmark 足够支撑）

**Abstract**:
- "per-example analysis on LAMBADA and ARC-Easy" → "per-example analysis on four benchmarks: LAMBADA, BoolQ, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy"
- 加入 multiplicity spectrum: "ambiguity α ranges from 0.10 (HellaSwag) to 0.49 (BoolQ), forming a task-dependent spectrum"
- "across two task categories (retrieval and mixed)" → "across all four task categories (retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed)"

**§3.2 Method**: 标注 BoolQ/HellaSwag 也做 per-example 分析（之前只列为 aggregate benchmarks）

**§4.3 Per-Example Divergence**:
- 扩展 Table 2 为 4-benchmark 版本（按上面汇总表格式）
- "Cross-task generalization" paragraph → 重写为 "Multiplicity spectrum"，呈现 4 benchmark 的 α 变化
- HellaSwag α=0.10 的解释：reasoning tasks 90% 样本跨架构一致，说明 multiplicity 不是 noise artifact
- BoolQ α=0.49 的解释：comprehension tasks 最敏感，接近一半样本有架构分歧
- Jaccard 全部 ~0.08-0.10：低重叠是 universal pattern，不依赖于 α 高低

**§4.4 Decoupling**: 提及 HellaSwag 也符合 decoupling pattern（ρ=-0.80 趋向 convergence 但 Jaccard 仍低）

**Introduction**:
- Finding 3: 升级为 "across all four task categories" + multiplicity spectrum
- Contribution #3: "observed on four benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed tasks (α = 0.10–0.49)"

**Conclusion**: 更新为 4-benchmark evidence + spectrum narrative

**Author Response Letter**: 更新 W-GENERAL 回应，标注 4-benchmark 扩展

**ARR Checklist A3**: 更新 claim scope

- HellaSwag 使用 acc_norm（不是 acc），在 Method 中注明
- GDN h24-1 BoolQ acc=0.489 接近随机，这是真实表现——分析中注意
- 页数约束：8 页主文。新增 Table 不占太多空间（4 行 vs 原 2 行），但 §4.3 扩展需要压缩其他地方

51	
52	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
53	
54	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.
55	
56	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.104$ for helped, $\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\max} = \min(|A|,|B|)/\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
57	
58	No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \citep{damour2022underspecification}.
59	
60	\paragraph{Cross-task generalization.} We repeat the analysis on ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), which shows no aggregate convergence ($\rho = -0.20$, $p = .783$). Pairwise Jaccard ranges from 0.067 to 0.134 (all 24 pair $\times$ ratio combinations $p < .01$). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} is substantial on both benchmarks ($\alpha \approx 0.21$--$0.30$; Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) and stable across attention ratios, confirming that multiplicity is not contingent on aggregate convergence \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.
61	
62	\begin{table}[t]
63	\centering
64	\small
65	\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B (h3-1). $\alpha$: ambiguity \citep{marx2020predictive}. $\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets.}
66	\label{tab:multiplicity}
67	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}
68	\toprule
69	 & $N$ & $\alpha$ & $\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\rho_\sigma$ \\
70	\midrule
71	LAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .10 & .09 & $-1.0^*$ \\
72	ARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .10 & .11 & $-0.2$ \\
73	\bottomrule
74	\end{tabular}
75	\par\vspace{1pt}
76	{\footnotesize $^*\!p = .017$. $\alpha$ stable across ratios (.29--.32 / .20--.23).}
77	\end{table}
78	
79	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
80	
81	The central finding of this paper is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
82	
83	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%.
84	
85	This separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) confirm this statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly three times the cross-architecture value, confirming that architecture identity has greater influence than attention dose on which examples are affected.
86	
87	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) provides partial insight: GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions at layer 2 (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples are negligible ($|\Delta H| \leq 0.018$), suggesting per-example effects originate in the recurrent layers. On ARC-Easy, where no aggregate convergence occurs ($\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), ambiguity remains comparable ($\alpha \approx 0.21$ vs.\ 0.30; Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}), confirming independence from aggregate equalization.
88	
89	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
90	
91	Removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly confounded with reduced statistical resolution at $n = 2$ architectures. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ (${\approx}$54\% below pure) while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108, confirming the result is not an artifact of the GDN head-count confound (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
92	
93	The confound is specific: GDN's h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations, introducing an attention-capacity asymmetry. The aggregate convergence result is therefore partially dependent on GDN; the decoupling result is not.
94

Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%.\n\nThis separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) confirm this statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly three times the cross-architecture value, confirming that architecture identity has greater influence than attention dose on which examples are affected.\n\nAttention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) provides partial insight: GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions at layer 2 (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples are negligible ($|\\Delta H| \\leq 0.018$), suggesting per-example effects originate in the recurrent layers. On ARC-Easy, where no aggregate convergence occurs ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), ambiguity remains comparable ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$ vs.\\ 0.30; Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}), confirming independence from aggregate equalization.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN weakens the LAMBADA convergence from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly confounded with reduced statistical resolution at $n = 2$ architectures. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\\sigma \\leq 0.008$ (${\\approx}$54\\% below pure) while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108, confirming the result is not an artifact of the GDN head-count confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n\nThe confound is specific: GDN's h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations, introducing an attention-capacity asymmetry. The aggregate convergence result is therefore partially dependent on GDN; the decoupling result is not.\n",

1	\documentclass[11pt]{article}
2	
3	\usepackage[review]{acl}

6	\usepackage{latexsym}

17	\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures}
18	
19	\author{Anonymous}
20	
21	\begin{document}
22	\maketitle
23	
24	\begin{abstract}
25	Hybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention layers with linear recurrence are increasingly standard, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across different recurrent backbones remains poorly quantified. We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with per-example analysis on LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$). Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; $\sigma$ drops 79\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), complete at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M. Per-example predictive multiplicity, however, is general: on both benchmarks, architectures help and hurt different examples when attention is added (Jaccard $\leq 0.13$, all $p < .01$; ambiguity $\alpha \approx 0.21$--$0.30$ stable across attention ratios). On LAMBADA, aggregate and per-example trajectories are decoupled: $\sigma$ decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at 4\% attention. On ARC-Easy, per-example divergence persists without any aggregate convergence ($\rho = -0.20$, n.s.). These results establish predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} across two task categories (retrieval and mixed) in three linear recurrent backbones, independent of aggregate performance equalization.
26	\end{abstract}
27	
28	\input{sections/introduction}
29	
30	\input{sections/related_work}
31	
32	\input{sections/method}
33	
34	\input{sections/experiments}
35	
36	\input{sections/conclusion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our analysis has several limitations that constrain the scope of its conclusions.
41	
42	\paragraph{Statistical power.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value under an exact permutation test is approximately 0.008. This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. We report confidence intervals as a complementary characterization to partially address this constraint.
43	
44	\paragraph{Per-example scope.} Our per-example analysis covers two benchmarks (LAMBADA, ARC-Easy) spanning retrieval and mixed-reasoning tasks. Extending to additional benchmarks and task types would strengthen the generality claim, particularly for comprehension tasks (e.g., BoolQ) where different per-example patterns may emerge.
45	
46	\paragraph{Scale coverage.} We compare two scale points (340M and 1.3B parameters). The contrast between complete convergence at 1.3B and partial convergence at 340M is a binary observation, not a scaling law. Intermediate and larger scales would be needed to characterize the functional relationship between model capacity and convergence.
47	
48	\paragraph{Architecture coverage.} Our study examines three recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) from one model zoo. Other hybrid families, including Jamba, RWKV-6, and Hymba, use different mixing strategies that may produce different per-example patterns. Generalization beyond these three architectures requires additional controlled experiments. Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.
49	
50	\paragraph{Observational design.} Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal. We report cosine similarities and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples but cannot determine whether attention patterns cause the per-example effects or merely correlate with them. Interventional methods such as attention head ablation would be necessary to establish causal links.
51	
52	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models in our study are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning. Per-example effects may change under task-specific adaptation, and the decoupling pattern we observe may not persist after fine-tuning.
53	
54	\section*{Ethics Statement}
55	
56	This work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.
57	
58	\input{arr_checklist}
59	
60	\bibliography{references}
61	
62	\appendix
63	\input{sections/appendix}
64	
65	\end{document}
66

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	When attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\% reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\% CI excludes zero). Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right. This tension between aggregate agreement and per-example disagreement arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
4	
5	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
6	
7	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\% to 25\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}
12	\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat.}
13	\label{fig:decoupling}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Our analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\% net reduction versus 79\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both benchmarks---a retrieval task (LAMBADA) and a mixed task (ARC-Easy)---in three recurrent backbones, independent of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while pairwise Jaccard of per-example effects saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.095$)---the two quantities are decoupled. On ARC-Easy, no aggregate convergence occurs ($\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), yet per-example overlap is comparably low (Jaccard 0.07--0.13, all $p < .01$) and the ambiguity $\alpha$ of \citet{marx2020predictive} is 0.21. Across both benchmarks, $\alpha$ is stable at 0.21--0.30 regardless of attention ratio.
17	
18	Our contributions are as follows:
19	\begin{itemize}
20	    \item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}).
21	    \item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\S\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).
22	    \item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both a retrieval task and a mixed task in three recurrent backbones (ambiguity $\alpha \approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of whether aggregate performance converges (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}).
23	\end{itemize}
24	
```

These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\\% net reduction versus 79\\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both benchmarks---a retrieval task (LAMBADA) and a mixed task (ARC-Easy)---in three recurrent backbones, independent of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while pairwise Jaccard of per-example effects saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.095$)---the two quantities are decoupled. On ARC-Easy, no aggregate convergence occurs ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), yet per-example overlap is comparably low (Jaccard 0.07--0.13, all $p < .01$) and the ambiguity $\\alpha$ of \\citet{marx2020predictive} is 0.21. Across both benchmarks, $\\alpha$ is stable at 0.21--0.30 regardless of attention ratio.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\\S\\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both a retrieval task and a mixed task in three recurrent backbones (ambiguity $\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of whether aggregate performance converges (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
      "numLines": 24,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 24
    }

```text
28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples) and ARC-Easy (2{,}376 examples). For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid and classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means attention produces more correct predictions than it removes; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.
45	
46	\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\rho| \geq |\rho_{\text{obs}}|$.
47	
48	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
49	
50	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.
51	
52	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.
53	
54	\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~D.
55	
```

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\\% pure recurrent, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).\n\n\\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\\label{ssec:task_convergence}\n\nWe first ask whether adding attention layers reduces performance differences between architectures, and whether any such reduction is uniform across tasks. For each benchmark, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation ($\\sigma$) at each attention ratio and measure the Spearman rank correlation ($\\rho$) between attention ratio and $\\sigma$ across the five configurations.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:convergence} reports results for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\\rho = -1.0$ (exact permutation test, $p = .017$, two-tailed, $n = 5$, 120 permutations). Cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction. The 95\\% BCa percentile bootstrap confidence interval for $\\rho$ is $[-1.0, -0.7]$ (10{,}000 resamples), which excludes zero. After Bonferroni correction for six confirmatory benchmarks, the adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict Bonferroni correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$. Benjamini--Hochberg false discovery rate control yields a similar adjusted $p$ (0.102), as only one of six benchmarks approaches nominal significance. The confidence interval provides a complementary characterization of the convergence effect, indicating a reliably negative association even though the corrected $p$-value does not reach conventional significance.\n\nLAMBADA is a pure context-retrieval task \\citep{paperno2016lambada}; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, consistent with convergence reflecting compensation for differences in how recurrent architectures store contextual information. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$, $p \\geq .233$). ARC-Easy and MMLU exhibit moderate negative correlations ($\\rho = -0.7$) but non-monotonic trajectories; COPA shows a similar pattern ($\\rho = -0.67$) but is limited by $n = 100$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). The decoupling analysis (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) requires a converging benchmark and is demonstrated on LAMBADA; per-example divergence itself extends to ARC-Easy (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.3$ & .683 & 1.17 & 1.18 & 0.99 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \\times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Scale-Dependent Convergence}\\label{ssec:scale_convergence}\n\nWe compare the LAMBADA convergence pattern at two scales. At 1.3B parameters, convergence is monotonic: $\\sigma$ decreases at every attention level from 0\\% through 25\\% ($\\rho = -1.0$). At 340M, convergence is partial. $\\sigma$ reaches a maximum reduction of 56\\% at intermediate attention ratios, but rebounds at h3-1, yielding a net endpoint reduction of 36\\%: the trajectory is non-monotonic, and the highest-attention model does not achieve the lowest cross-architecture variance. One possible interpretation is that at smaller scale, the capacity overhead of attention layers creates interference that offsets the retrieval benefit.\n\nThis contrast is a preliminary observation from two data points (not a scaling law) that scale may facilitate complete equalization. Despite the aggregate divergence, mean pairwise Jaccard similarity is similar at both scales (340M: $J = 0.094$ helped, $0.090$ hurt; 1.3B: $0.100$, $0.093$), extending the decoupling observation (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) to the scale dimension.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nDo architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.062 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\nNo surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors, but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising from architectural variation rather than random initialization \\citep{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-task generalization.} We repeat the analysis on ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), which shows no aggregate convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, $p = .783$). Pairwise Jaccard ranges from 0.067 to 0.134 (all 24 pair $\\times$ ratio combinations $p < .01$). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} is substantial on both benchmarks ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$; Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) and stable across attention ratios, confirming that multiplicity is not contingent on aggregate convergence \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B (h3-1). $\\alpha$: ambiguity \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\sigma$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .10 & .09 & $-1.0^*$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .10 & .11 & $-0.2$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^*\\!p = .017$. $\\alpha$ stable across ratios (.29--.32 / .20--.23).}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nThe central finding of this paper is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%.\n\nThis separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples) confirm this statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly three times the cross-architecture value, confirming that architecture identity has greater influence than attention dose on which examples are affected.\n\nAttention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) provides partial insight: GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions at layer 2 (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples are negligible ($|\\Delta H| \\leq 0.018$), suggesting per-example effects originate in the recurrent layers. On ARC-Easy, where no aggregate convergence occurs ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), ambiguity remains comparable ($\\alpha \\approx 0.21$ vs.\\ 0.30; Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}), confirming independence from aggregate equalization.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN weakens the LAMBADA convergence from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly confounded with reduced statistical resolution at $n = 2$ architectures. The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\\sigma \\leq 0.008$ (${\\approx}$54\\% below pure) while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108, confirming the result is not an artifact of the GDN head-count confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n\nThe confound is specific: GDN's h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations, introducing an attention-capacity asymmetry. The aggregate convergence result is therefore partially dependent on GDN; the decoupling result is not.\n",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are added to three different recurrent architectures, their LAMBADA performance becomes near-identical: cross-architecture standard deviation drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero). Yet this aggregate convergence hides an asymmetry at the per-example level. The three architectures reach similar overall accuracy by helping and hurting different examples with low overlap (pairwise Jaccard at most 0.11, approximately twice the independence baseline). Two models can agree on a benchmark score while disagreeing on which inputs they get right. This tension between aggregate agreement and per-example disagreement arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$), we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields three findings. First, attention-driven convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. Second, convergence depends on scale. At 1.3B parameters the $\\sigma$ trajectory is monotonically decreasing, but at 340M it is non-monotonic: $\\sigma$ partially rebounds at the highest attention ratio, with only 36\\% net reduction versus 79\\% at 1.3B. This contrast provides a preliminary observation that sufficient model capacity may facilitate complete equalization on retrieval. Third, per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both benchmarks---a retrieval task (LAMBADA) and a mixed task (ARC-Easy)---in three recurrent backbones, independent of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while pairwise Jaccard of per-example effects saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.095$)---the two quantities are decoupled. On ARC-Easy, no aggregate convergence occurs ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.), yet per-example overlap is comparably low (Jaccard 0.07--0.13, all $p < .01$) and the ambiguity $\\alpha$ of \\citet{marx2020predictive} is 0.21. Across both benchmarks, $\\alpha$ is stable at 0.21--0.30 regardless of attention ratio.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We quantify task-specific convergence: only retrieval tasks (LAMBADA) show attention-driven cross-architecture convergence at 1.3B scale; reasoning and comprehension benchmarks do not (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}, Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}, Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}).\n    \\item We observe scale-dependent convergence: the monotonic convergence pattern at 1.3B becomes non-monotonic at 340M, a preliminary observation that sufficient scale may facilitate complete equalization (\\S\\ref{ssec:scale_convergence}).\n    \\item We demonstrate that per-example predictive multiplicity is observed on both a retrieval task and a mixed task in three recurrent backbones (ambiguity $\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of whether aggregate performance converges (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 24,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 24
      }

We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with per-example analysis on LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$) and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$). Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), complete at 1.3B but non-monotonic at 340M. Per-example predictive multiplicity, however, is general: on both benchmarks, architectures help and hurt different examples when attention is added (Jaccard $\\leq 0.13$, all $p < .01$; ambiguity $\\alpha \\approx 0.21$--$0.30$ stable across attention ratios). On LAMBADA, aggregate and per-example trajectories are decoupled: $\\sigma$ decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention. On ARC-Easy, per-example divergence persists without any aggregate convergence ($\\rho = -0.20$, n.s.). These results establish predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} across two task categories (retrieval and mixed) in three linear recurrent backbones, independent of aggregate performance equalization.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations that constrain the scope of its conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value under an exact permutation test is approximately 0.008. This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. We report confidence intervals as a complementary characterization to partially address this constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-example scope.} Our per-example analysis covers two benchmarks (LAMBADA, ARC-Easy) spanning retrieval and mixed-reasoning tasks. Extending to additional benchmarks and task types would strengthen the generality claim, particularly for comprehension tasks (e.g., BoolQ) where different per-example patterns may emerge.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale coverage.} We compare two scale points (340M and 1.3B parameters). The contrast between complete convergence at 1.3B and partial convergence at 340M is a binary observation, not a scaling law. Intermediate and larger scales would be needed to characterize the functional relationship between model capacity and convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture coverage.} Our study examines three recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) from one model zoo. Other hybrid families, including Jamba, RWKV-6, and Hymba, use different mixing strategies that may produce different per-example patterns. Generalization beyond these three architectures requires additional controlled experiments. Each configuration in the model zoo corresponds to a single training run; we cannot disentangle architectural effects from seed-specific effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Observational design.} Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal. We report cosine similarities and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples but cannot determine whether attention patterns cause the per-example effects or merely correlate with them. Interventional methods such as attention head ablation would be necessary to establish causal links.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models in our study are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning. Per-example effects may change under task-specific adaptation, and the decoupling pattern we observe may not persist after fine-tuning.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\input{arr_checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
        "numLines": 66,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 66
      }
    },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011k41QFo6y9kuHku6pexAnK`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Aggregate convergence is task-specific (only LAMBADA among ten benchmarks) and scale-dependent (monotonic at 1.3B, non-monotonic at 340M). Per-example predictive multiplicity, however, is observed on both a retrieval task (LAMBADA) and a mixed task (ARC-Easy) in three architectures ($\alpha \approx 0.21$--$0.30$), independent of aggregate convergence. The low per-example overlap (Jaccard $\leq 0.14$) suggests that, if confirmed in additional architectures and benchmarks, diverse hybrid ensembles may provide complementary coverage. Future work should establish causal mechanisms through attention ablation and extend the analysis beyond these three architectures.
4	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RyN9B3bH1aVwcFrBhbY7QH`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
6	
7	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\% (pure recurrent), 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, and 25\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\%, h12-1 to 7.7\%, and h24-1 to 4\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.
8	
9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
15	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
16	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark.
17	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	All evaluations use the lm-evaluation-harness framework \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks.
21	
22	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
23	
24	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
25	
26	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that attention reduces cross-architecture variance.
27	
28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples) and ARC-Easy (2{,}376 examples). For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid and classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means attention produces more correct predictions than it removes; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.
45	
46	\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\rho| \geq |\rho_{\text{obs}}|$.
47	
48	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
49	
50	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.
51	
52	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.
53	
54	\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~D.
55

Per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude but is present on all four benchmarks: the ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.10 (HellaSwag) to 0.49 (BoolQ), forming a task-dependent spectrum rather than a binary phenomenon, while pairwise Jaccard of helped/hurt sets remains universally low ($\\leq 0.13$, all $p < .01$). On LAMBADA, aggregate and per-example trajectories are decoupled: $\\sigma$ decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at 4\\% attention. These results establish per-example predictive multiplicity across all four task categories (retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, mixed) in three linear recurrent backbones, with HellaSwag's near-zero $\\alpha$ serving as a reference point that rules out measurement noise."
}
```

This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\input{arr_checklist}\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
